<p><b>John George Diefenbaker</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" title="Order of the Companions of Honour">CH</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_Counsel" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen&#39;s Counsel">QC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society_of_Canada" title="Fellow of the Royal Society of Canada">FRSC</a>&#32;</span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;d&#39; in &#39;dye&#39;">d</span><span title="/iː/: &#39;ee&#39; in &#39;fleece&#39;">iː</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="/ən/: &#39;on&#39; in &#39;button&#39;">ən</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">DEE</span>-fən-bay-kər</i></a>; September 18, 1895 – August 16, 1979) was the 13th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a>, serving from 1957 to 1963. He was the only <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative</a><sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> party leader between 1930 and 1979 to lead the party to an election victory, doing so three times, although only once with a <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority of the seats</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_House_of_Commons" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian House of Commons">House of Commons</a>.
</p><p>Diefenbaker was born in the small town of <a href="/wiki/Neustadt,_Ontario" title="Neustadt, Ontario">Neustadt</a> in <a href="/wiki/Southwestern_Ontario" title="Southwestern Ontario">Southwestern Ontario</a>. In 1903, his family migrated west to the portion of the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territories" title="Northwest Territories">North-West Territories</a> that would soon become the province of <a href="/wiki/Saskatchewan" title="Saskatchewan">Saskatchewan</a>. He grew up in the province and was interested in politics from a young age. After service in <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, Diefenbaker became a noted criminal defence lawyer. He contested elections through the 1920s and 1930s with little success until he was finally elected to the House of Commons in <a href="/wiki/1940_Canadian_federal_election" title="1940 Canadian federal election">1940</a>.
</p><p>Diefenbaker was repeatedly a candidate for the party leadership. He gained that position in <a href="/wiki/1956_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention" title="1956 Progressive Conservative leadership convention">1956</a>, on his third attempt. In <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957</a>, he led the party to its first electoral victory in 27 years; a year later he called a <a href="/wiki/Snap_election" title="Snap election">snap election</a> and spearheaded them to one of their <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">greatest triumphs</a>. Diefenbaker appointed the first female minister in Canadian history to his cabinet (<a href="/wiki/Ellen_Fairclough" title="Ellen Fairclough">Ellen Fairclough</a>), as well as the first <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Canada" title="Indigenous peoples in Canada">Indigenous</a> member of the <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a> (<a href="/wiki/James_Gladstone" title="James Gladstone">James Gladstone</a>). During his six years as prime minister, his government obtained passage of the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Bill_of_Rights" title="Canadian Bill of Rights">Canadian Bill of Rights</a></i> and granted the vote to the <a href="/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada" title="First Nations in Canada">First Nations</a> and <a href="/wiki/Inuit" title="Inuit">Inuit</a> peoples. In 1962, Diefenbaker's government eliminated racial discrimination in immigration policy. In foreign policy, his stance against <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a> helped secure the departure of <a href="/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa" title="Union of South Africa">South Africa</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>, but his <a href="/wiki/Bomarc_Missile_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Bomarc Missile Crisis">indecision on whether to accept Bomarc nuclear missiles</a> from the United States led to his government's downfall. Diefenbaker is also remembered for his role in the 1959 cancellation of the <a href="/wiki/CF-105_Arrow" class="mw-redirect" title="CF-105 Arrow">Avro Arrow</a> project.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1962_Canadian_federal_election" title="1962 Canadian federal election">1962 federal election</a>, the Progressive Conservatives narrowly won a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> before losing power altogether in <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">1963</a>. Diefenbaker stayed on as party leader, becoming <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">Opposition leader</a>, but his <a href="/wiki/1965_Canadian_federal_election" title="1965 Canadian federal election">second loss at the polls</a> prompted opponents within the party to force him to <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1967" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1967">a leadership convention in 1967</a>. Diefenbaker stood for re-election as party leader at the last moment, but attracted only minimal support and withdrew. He remained in parliament until his death in 1979, two months after <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a> became the first Progressive Conservative prime minister since Diefenbaker. Diefenbaker ranks average in <a href="/wiki/Rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Rankings of prime ministers of Canada">rankings of prime ministers of Canada</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg/220px-John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg/330px-John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg/440px-John_Diefenbaker%27s_Birthplace.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a><figcaption>John Diefenbaker's birthplace</figcaption></figure>
<p>Diefenbaker was born on September 18, 1895, in <a href="/wiki/Neustadt,_Ontario" title="Neustadt, Ontario">Neustadt, Ontario</a>, to William Thomas Diefenbaker and Mary Florence Diefenbaker, née Bannerman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith19951_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith19951-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> His father was the son of German immigrants from Adersbach (near <a href="/wiki/Sinsheim" title="Sinsheim">Sinsheim</a>) in Baden; Mary Diefenbaker was of Scottish descent and Diefenbaker was Baptist.<sup id="cite_ref-anglicized_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-anglicized-3">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> The family moved to several locations in Ontario in John's early years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith19951_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith19951-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> William Diefenbaker was a teacher, and had deep interests in history and politics, which he sought to inculcate in his students. He had remarkable success doing so; of the 28 students at his school near Toronto in 1903, four, including his son, John, served as Conservative MPs in the <a href="/wiki/19th_Canadian_Parliament" title="19th Canadian Parliament">19th Canadian Parliament</a> beginning in 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker197517–18_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker197517–18-4">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>—the others were <a href="/wiki/Robert_Henry_McGregor" title="Robert Henry McGregor">Robert Henry McGregor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Henry_Harris" title="Joseph Henry Harris">Joseph Henry Harris</a>, and <a href="/wiki/George_Tustin" title="George Tustin">George Tustin</a>.
</p><p>The Diefenbaker family moved west in 1903, for William Diefenbaker to accept a position near <a href="/wiki/Fort_Carlton" title="Fort Carlton">Fort Carlton</a>, then in the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territories" title="Northwest Territories">Northwest Territories</a> (now in Saskatchewan).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith19955–6_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith19955–6-5">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In 1906, William claimed a <a href="/wiki/Dominion_Land_Survey" title="Dominion Land Survey">quarter-section</a>, 160 acres (0.65&#160;km<sup>2</sup>) of undeveloped land near <a href="/wiki/Borden,_Saskatchewan" title="Borden, Saskatchewan">Borden, Saskatchewan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196315_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196315-6">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In February 1910, the Diefenbaker family moved to <a href="/wiki/Saskatoon" title="Saskatoon">Saskatoon</a>, the site of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Saskatchewan" title="University of Saskatchewan">University of Saskatchewan</a>. William and Mary Diefenbaker felt that John and his brother Elmer would have greater educational opportunities in Saskatoon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196316_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196316-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Diefenbaker had been interested in politics from an early age and told his mother at the age of eight or nine that he would some day be prime minister. She told him that it was an impossible ambition, especially for a boy living on the prairies.<sup id="cite_ref-childhood_ambition_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-childhood_ambition-8">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> She would live to be proved wrong.<sup id="cite_ref-childhood_ambition_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-childhood_ambition-8">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> John claimed that his first contact with politics came in 1910, when he sold a newspaper to Prime Minister Sir <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>, in Saskatoon to lay the cornerstone for the university's first building. The present and future Prime Ministers conversed, and when giving his speech that afternoon, Sir Wilfrid commented on the newsboy who had ended their conversation by saying, "I can't waste any more time on you, Prime Minister. I must get about my work."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196316_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196316-7">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-phrasing_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-phrasing-9">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> The authenticity of the meeting was questioned in the 21st century, with an author suggesting that it was invented by Diefenbaker during an election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a 1977 interview with the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Canadian Broadcasting Corporation">CBC</a>, Diefenbaker recalled he saw injustice first-hand in his youth against <a href="/wiki/French_Canadians" title="French Canadians">French Canadians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Canadians" class="mw-redirect" title="Indigenous Canadians">Indigenous Canadians</a> and the <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A9tis" title="Métis">Métis</a>. He said, "From my earliest days, I knew the meaning of discrimination. Many Canadians were virtually second-hand citizens because of their names and racial origin. Indeed, it seemed until the end of World War II that the only first-class Canadians were either of English or French descent. As a youth, l determined to devote myself to assuring that all Canadians, whatever their racial origin, were equal and declared myself to be a sworn enemy of discrimination."<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker_(ca_1919).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Formal photo portrait of Diefenbaker at age 23. His short dark hair is very thick and curly and has been combed back smoothly, and his face is thinner than at an older age. He is formally dressed with a stiffened shirt collar and wears an academic hood. Picture is inscribed &quot;J.G. Diefenbaker, law&quot;." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/71/John_Diefenbaker_%28ca_1919%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="165" height="290" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="165" data-file-height="290" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker as a law student, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr>&#160;1919</figcaption></figure>
<p>After graduating from high school in Saskatoon in 1912, Diefenbaker entered the University of Saskatchewan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199515–16_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199515–16-14">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He received his Bachelor of Arts degree in 1915, and his <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts" title="Master of Arts">Master of Arts</a> the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199519–20_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199519–20-15">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker was commissioned a lieutenant into the <a href="/wiki/196th_(Western_Universities)_Battalion,_CEF" class="mw-redirect" title="196th (Western Universities) Battalion, CEF">196th (Western Universities) Battalion, CEF</a><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> in May 1916. In September of that year, Diefenbaker was part of a contingent of 300 junior officers sent to Britain for pre-deployment training. Diefenbaker related in his memoirs that he was hit by a shovel, and the injury eventually resulted in him being sent home as an invalid. Diefenbaker's recollections do not correspond with his army medical records, which show no contemporary account of such an injury, and his biographer, Denis Smith, speculates that any injury was <a href="/wiki/Psychosomatic" class="mw-redirect" title="Psychosomatic">psychosomatic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199520–30_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199520–30-18">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After leaving the military in 1917,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker returned to Saskatchewan where he resumed his work as an <a href="/wiki/Articled_clerk" title="Articled clerk">articling student</a> in law. He received his law degree in 1919,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199531–33_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199531–33-19">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> the first student to secure three degrees from the University of Saskatchewan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker197579_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker197579-20">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> On June 30, 1919, he was <a href="/wiki/Call_to_the_Bar" class="mw-redirect" title="Call to the Bar">called to the bar</a>, and the following day, opened a small practice in the village of <a href="/wiki/Wakaw,_Saskatchewan" class="mw-redirect" title="Wakaw, Saskatchewan">Wakaw, Saskatchewan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199531–33_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199531–33-19">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Barrister_and_candidate_.281919.E2.80.931940.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Barrister_and_candidate_(1919–1940)">Barrister and candidate (1919–1940)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Barrister and candidate (1919–1940)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Electoral_history_of_John_Diefenbaker" title="Electoral history of John Diefenbaker">Electoral history of John Diefenbaker</a></div>
<h3><span id="Wakaw_days_.281919.E2.80.931924.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Wakaw_days_(1919–1924)">Wakaw days (1919–1924)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Wakaw days (1919–1924)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dief_shack.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Small wooden shack, painted white, with sign &quot;J.G. Diefenbaker Law Office 1919–1925" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Dief_shack.jpg/220px-Dief_shack.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Dief_shack.jpg/330px-Dief_shack.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Dief_shack.jpg/440px-Dief_shack.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption>Recreation of Diefenbaker's first office, Wakaw, Saskatchewan</figcaption></figure>
<p>Although Wakaw had a population of only 400, it sat at the heart of a densely populated area of rural <a href="/wiki/Township_(Canada)#Western_Canada" title="Township (Canada)">townships</a> and had its own <a href="/wiki/Provincial_Court_of_Saskatchewan" title="Provincial Court of Saskatchewan">district court</a>. It was also easily accessible to Saskatoon, <a href="/wiki/Prince_Albert,_Saskatchewan" title="Prince Albert, Saskatchewan">Prince Albert</a> and <a href="/wiki/Humboldt,_Saskatchewan" title="Humboldt, Saskatchewan">Humboldt</a>, places where the <a href="/wiki/Court_of_Queen%27s_Bench_for_Saskatchewan" class="mw-redirect" title="Court of Queen&#39;s Bench for Saskatchewan">Court of King's Bench</a> sat. The local people were mostly immigrants, and Diefenbaker's research found them to be particularly litigious. There was already one barrister in town, and the residents were loyal to him, initially refusing to rent office space to Diefenbaker. The new lawyer was forced to rent a vacant lot and erect a two-room wooden shack.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199534–35_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199534–35-21">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker won the local people over through his success; in his first year in practice, he tried 62 jury trials, winning approximately half of his cases. He rarely called defence witnesses, thereby avoiding the possibility of rebuttal witnesses for the Crown, and securing the last word for himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196318_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196318-22">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In late 1920, he was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Town_council" title="Town council">village council</a> to serve a three-year term.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199538_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199538-23">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker would often spend weekends with his parents in Saskatoon. While there, he began to woo <a href="/wiki/Olive_Diefenbaker" title="Olive Diefenbaker">Olive Freeman</a>, daughter of the Baptist minister, but in 1921, she moved with her family to <a href="/wiki/Brandon,_Manitoba" title="Brandon, Manitoba">Brandon, Manitoba</a>, and the two lost touch for more than 20 years. He then courted Beth Newell, a cashier in Saskatoon, and by 1922, the two were engaged. However, in 1923, Newell was diagnosed with <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a>, and Diefenbaker broke off contact with her. She died the following year. Diefenbaker was himself subject to internal bleeding, and may have feared that the disease would be transmitted to him. In late 1923, he had an operation at the <a href="/wiki/Mayo_Clinic" title="Mayo Clinic">Mayo Clinic</a> for a <a href="/wiki/Gastric_ulcer" class="mw-redirect" title="Gastric ulcer">gastric ulcer</a>, but his health remained uncertain for several more years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199538–39_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199538–39-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After four years in Wakaw, Diefenbaker so dominated the local legal practice that his competitor left town. On May 1, 1924, Diefenbaker moved to Prince Albert, leaving a law partner in charge of the Wakaw office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199541_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199541-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Aspiring_politician_.281924.E2.80.931929.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Aspiring_politician_(1924–1929)">Aspiring politician (1924–1929)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Aspiring politician (1924–1929)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Since 1905, when Saskatchewan entered <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a>, the province had been dominated by the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Saskatchewan" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Saskatchewan">Liberal Party</a>, which practised highly effective <a href="/wiki/Machine_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="Machine politics">machine politics</a>. Diefenbaker was fond of stating, in his later years, that the only protection a Conservative had in the province was that afforded by the <a href="/wiki/Game_laws" class="mw-redirect" title="Game laws">game laws</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker197564_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker197564-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker's father, William, was a Liberal; however, John Diefenbaker found himself attracted to the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historic)">Conservative Party</a>. Free trade was widely popular throughout Western Canada, but Diefenbaker was convinced by the Conservative position that free trade would make Canada an economic dependent of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199541–42_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199541–42-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> However, he did not speak publicly of his politics. Diefenbaker recalled in his memoirs that, in 1921, he had been elected as secretary of the Wakaw Liberal Association while absent in Saskatoon, and had returned to find the association's records in his office. He promptly returned them to the association president. Diefenbaker also stated that he had been told that if he became a Liberal candidate, "there was no position in the province which would not be open to him."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199543_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199543-28">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was not until 1925 that Diefenbaker publicly came forward as a Conservative, a year in which both federal and Saskatchewan provincial elections were held. Journalist <a href="/wiki/Peter_C._Newman" title="Peter C. Newman">Peter C. Newman</a>, in his best-selling account of the Diefenbaker years, suggested that this choice was made for practical, rather than political reasons, as Diefenbaker had little chance of defeating established politicians and securing the Liberal nomination for either the House of Commons or the <a href="/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Saskatchewan" title="Legislative Assembly of Saskatchewan">Legislative Assembly</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196319–20_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196319–20-29">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/1925_Saskatchewan_general_election" title="1925 Saskatchewan general election">provincial election</a> took place in early June; Liberals would later claim that Diefenbaker had campaigned for their party in the election. On June 19, however, Diefenbaker addressed a Conservative organizing committee, and on August 6, was nominated as the party's candidate for the federal riding of <a href="/wiki/Prince_Albert_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Prince Albert (federal electoral district)">Prince Albert</a>, a district in which the party's last candidate had lost his <a href="/wiki/Deposit_(politics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Deposit (politics)">election deposit</a>. A nasty campaign ensued, in which Diefenbaker was called a "<a href="/wiki/List_of_terms_used_for_Germans#Hun" title="List of terms used for Germans">Hun</a>" because of his German-derived surname. The <a href="/wiki/1925_Canadian_federal_election" title="1925 Canadian federal election">1925 federal election</a> was held on October 29; he finished third behind the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Party of Canada">Progressive Party</a> candidates, losing his deposit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199544–46_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199544–46-30">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Election flyer naming Diefenbaker and with his photograph, with a recreation of the ballot, urging his election. His hair is still short and dark, and is combed back, and his face appears much the way it will in later years. He wears a jacket and tie." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg/220px-Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg/330px-Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg/440px-Diefenbaker_versus_Mackenzie_King.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3448" data-file-height="2057" /></a><figcaption>Handout for the Diefenbaker campaign 1926</figcaption></figure>
<p>The winning candidate, <a href="/wiki/Charles_McDonald_(Canadian_politician)" title="Charles McDonald (Canadian politician)">Charles McDonald</a>, did not hold the seat long, resigning it to open a place for the Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a>, who had been defeated in his Ontario riding. The Tories ran no candidate against King in the by-election on February 15, 1926, and he won easily. Although in the 1925 federal election, the Conservatives had won the greatest number of seats, King continued as Prime Minister with the support of the Progressives. Mackenzie King held office for several months until he finally resigned when the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Julian_Byng,_1st_Viscount_Byng_of_Vimy" title="Julian Byng, 1st Viscount Byng of Vimy">Lord Byng</a>, <a href="/wiki/King%E2%80%93Byng_Affair" class="mw-redirect" title="King–Byng Affair">refused a dissolution</a>. Conservative Party leader <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Meighen" title="Arthur Meighen">Arthur Meighen</a> became prime minister, but was quickly defeated in the House of Commons, and Byng finally granted a <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Dissolution of Parliament">dissolution of Parliament</a>. Diefenbaker, who had been confirmed as Conservative candidate, stood against King in the <a href="/wiki/1926_Canadian_federal_election" title="1926 Canadian federal election">1926 election</a>, a rare direct electoral contest between two individuals who had or would become prime minister. King triumphed easily over Diefenbaker, the Liberals won the federal election, and King regained his position as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199547–50_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199547–50-31">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Perennial_candidate_.281929.E2.80.931940.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Perennial_candidate_(1929–1940)">Perennial candidate (1929–1940)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Perennial candidate (1929–1940)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Diefenbaker with an intense look in his eyes. Still a young man, he wears ceremonial robes in this portrait shot; the bands of the robes are visible." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg/170px-Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="231" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg/255px-Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg/340px-Diefenbaker_as_King%27s_Counsel_1929.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1617" data-file-height="2196" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker as King's Counsel, 1929</figcaption></figure>
<p>Diefenbaker stood for the Legislative Assembly in the <a href="/wiki/1929_Saskatchewan_general_election" title="1929 Saskatchewan general election">1929 provincial election</a>. He was defeated,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199556–57_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199556–57-32">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> but Saskatchewan Conservatives formed their first government, with help from smaller parties. As the defeated Conservative candidate for <a href="/wiki/Prince_Albert_City" title="Prince Albert City">Prince Albert City</a>, he was given charge of political patronage there and was created a <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Counsel" title="King&#39;s Counsel">King's Counsel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199570–71_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199570–71-33">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Three weeks after his electoral defeat, he married Saskatoon teacher <a href="/wiki/Edna_Brower" class="mw-redirect" title="Edna Brower">Edna Brower</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199558–60_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199558–60-34">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker chose not to stand for the House of Commons in the <a href="/wiki/1930_Canadian_federal_election" title="1930 Canadian federal election">1930 federal election</a>, citing health reasons. The Conservatives gained a majority in the election, and party leader <a href="/wiki/R._B._Bennett" title="R. B. Bennett">R. B. Bennett</a> became prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199570–71_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199570–71-33">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker continued a high-profile legal practice, and in 1933, ran for mayor of Prince Albert. He was defeated by 48 votes in an election in which over 2,000 ballots were cast.<sup id="cite_ref-H._J._Fraser_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H._J._Fraser-35">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1934, when the Crown prosecutor for Prince Albert resigned to become the Conservative Party's legislative candidate, Diefenbaker took his place as prosecutor. Diefenbaker did not stand in the <a href="/wiki/1934_Saskatchewan_general_election" title="1934 Saskatchewan general election">1934 provincial election</a>, in which the governing Conservatives lost every seat. Six days after the election, Diefenbaker resigned as Crown prosecutor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199576–77_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199576–77-36">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The federal government of Bennett <a href="/wiki/1935_Canadian_federal_election" title="1935 Canadian federal election">was defeated the following year</a> and Mackenzie King returned as prime minister. Judging his prospects hopeless, Diefenbaker had declined a nomination to stand again against Mackenzie King in Prince Albert. In the waning days of the Bennett government, the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Saskatchewan" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Saskatchewan">Saskatchewan Conservative Party</a> president was appointed a judge, leaving Diefenbaker, who had been elected the party's vice president, as acting president of the provincial party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199581–83_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199581–83-37">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker_(1939_election_flyer).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Election poster naming Diefenbaker and with his photograph, with blank spaces for the time and place at which he would speak. His hair is still short and dark, and is combed back, and his face appears much the way it will in later years. He wears a jacket and tie." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/John_Diefenbaker_%281939_election_flyer%29.jpg/170px-John_Diefenbaker_%281939_election_flyer%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="344" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a6/John_Diefenbaker_%281939_election_flyer%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="504" /></a><figcaption>Poster to advertise a speech by John Diefenbaker as Conservative candidate, 1939 (leading up to the 1940 federal election)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Saskatchewan Conservatives eventually arranged a leadership convention for October 28, 1936. Eleven people were nominated, including Diefenbaker. The other ten candidates withdrew, and Diefenbaker won the position by default. Diefenbaker asked the federal party for $10,000 in financial support, but the funds were refused, and the Conservatives were shut out of the legislature in <a href="/wiki/1938_Saskatchewan_general_election" title="1938 Saskatchewan general election">the 1938 provincial elections</a> for the second consecutive time. Diefenbaker himself was defeated in the <a href="/wiki/Arm_River_(electoral_district)" title="Arm River (electoral district)">Arm River riding</a> by 190 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196322–23_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196322–23-38">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> With the province-wide Conservative vote having fallen to 12 percent, Diefenbaker offered his resignation to a post-election party meeting in <a href="/wiki/Moose_Jaw,_Saskatchewan" class="mw-redirect" title="Moose Jaw, Saskatchewan">Moose Jaw</a>, but it was refused. Diefenbaker continued to run the provincial party out of his law office and paid the party's debts from his own pocket.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith199598–99_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199598–99-39">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker quietly sought the Conservative nomination for the federal riding of <a href="/wiki/Lake_Centre_(electoral_district)" title="Lake Centre (electoral district)">Lake Centre</a> but was unwilling to risk a divisive intra-party squabble. In what Diefenbaker biographer Smith states "appears to have been an elaborate and prearranged charade", Diefenbaker attended the nominating convention as <a href="/wiki/Keynote_speaker" class="mw-redirect" title="Keynote speaker">keynote speaker</a>, but withdrew when his name was proposed, stating a local man should be selected. The winner among the six remaining candidates, riding president W. B. Kelly, declined the nomination, urging the delegates to select Diefenbaker, which they promptly did.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995102–103_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995102–103-40">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Mackenzie King called <a href="/wiki/1940_Canadian_federal_election" title="1940 Canadian federal election">a general election</a> for March 25, 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196323–24_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196323–24-41">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The incumbent in Lake Centre was Liberal <a href="/wiki/John_Frederick_Johnston" title="John Frederick Johnston">John Frederick Johnston</a>. Diefenbaker campaigned aggressively in Lake Centre, holding 63 rallies and seeking to appeal to members of all parties. On election day, he defeated Johnston by 280 votes on what was otherwise a disastrous day for the Conservatives, who won only 39 seats out of the 245 in the House of Commons—their lowest total since Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196323–24_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196323–24-41">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Parliamentary_rise_.281940.E2.80.931957.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Parliamentary_rise_(1940–1957)">Parliamentary rise (1940–1957)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Parliamentary rise (1940–1957)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Mackenzie_King_years_.281940.E2.80.931948.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mackenzie_King_years_(1940–1948)">Mackenzie King years (1940–1948)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Mackenzie King years (1940–1948)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Diefenbaker joined a shrunken and demoralized Conservative caucus in the House of Commons. The Conservative leader, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Manion" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Manion">Robert Manion</a>, failed to win a place in the Commons in the election, which saw the Liberals take 181 seats.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995109_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995109-42">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The Tories sought to be included in a wartime coalition government, but Mackenzie King refused. The House of Commons had only a slight role in <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">the war</a> effort; under the state of emergency, most business was accomplished through the Cabinet issuing <a href="/wiki/Order_in_Council" title="Order in Council">Orders in Council</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995116_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995116-43">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker was appointed to the House Committee on the <a href="/wiki/Defence_of_Canada_Regulations" title="Defence of Canada Regulations">Defence of Canada Regulations</a>, an all-party committee which examined the wartime rules which allowed arrest and detention without trial. On June 13, 1940, Diefenbaker made his <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> in the House of Commons, supporting the regulations, and emphatically stating that most Canadians of German descent were loyal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995114–115_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995114–115-44">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> In his memoirs, Diefenbaker wrote he waged an unsuccessful fight against the forced relocation and <a href="/wiki/Japanese_Canadian_internment" class="mw-redirect" title="Japanese Canadian internment">internment of many Japanese-Canadians</a>, but historians say that the fight against the internment never took place.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1975223–224_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1975223–224-45">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004194–195_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004194–195-46">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Diefenbaker's biographer, Denis Smith, the Conservative MP quietly admired Mackenzie King for his political skills.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995120–122_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995120–122-47">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> However, Diefenbaker proved a <a href="/wiki/Gadfly_(social)" class="mw-redirect" title="Gadfly (social)">gadfly</a> and an annoyance to Mackenzie King. Angered by the words of Diefenbaker and fellow Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/Howard_Charles_Green" title="Howard Charles Green">Howard Green</a> in seeking to censure the government, the Prime Minister referred to Conservative MPs as "a mob".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995120–122_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995120–122-47">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> When Diefenbaker accompanied two other Conservative leaders to a briefing by Mackenzie King on <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">the war</a>, the Prime Minister exploded at Diefenbaker (a constituent of his), "What business do you have to be here? You strike me to the heart every time you speak."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995120–122_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995120–122-47">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Conservatives elected a <a href="/wiki/Floor_leader" class="mw-redirect" title="Floor leader">floor leader</a>, and in 1941 approached former Prime Minister Meighen, who had been appointed as a senator by Bennett, about becoming party leader again. Meighen agreed, and resigned his Senate seat, but lost a by-election for an Ontario seat in the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995125_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995125-48">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> He remained as leader for several months, although he could not enter the chamber of the House of Commons. Meighen sought to move the Tories to the left, in order to undercut the Liberals and to take support away from the <a href="/wiki/Co-operative_Commonwealth_Federation" title="Co-operative Commonwealth Federation">Co-operative Commonwealth Federation</a> (CCF, the predecessor of the <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="New Democratic Party (Canada)">New Democratic Party</a> (NDP)). To that end, he sought to draft the <a href="/wiki/Liberal-Progressive#Manitoba" title="Liberal-Progressive">Liberal-Progressive</a> premier of <a href="/wiki/Manitoba" title="Manitoba">Manitoba</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Bracken" title="John Bracken">John Bracken</a>, to lead the Conservatives. Diefenbaker objected to what he saw as an attempt to rig the party's choice of new leader<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995128–129_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995128–129-49">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> and stood for the leadership himself at the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1942" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1942">party's 1942 leadership convention</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995130_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995130-50">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Bracken was elected on the second ballot; Diefenbaker finished a distant third in both polls. At Bracken's request, the convention changed the party's name to "Progressive Conservative Party of Canada."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995131–134_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995131–134-51">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Bracken chose not to seek entry to the House through a by-election, and when the Conservatives elected a new floor leader, Diefenbaker was defeated by one vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995135–136_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995135–136-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bracken was elected to the Commons in the <a href="/wiki/1945_Canadian_federal_election" title="1945 Canadian federal election">1945 general election</a>, and for the first time in five years the Tories had their party leader in the House of Commons. The Progressive Conservatives won 67 seats to the Liberals' 125, with smaller parties and independents winning 52 seats. Diefenbaker increased his majority to over 1,000 votes, and had the satisfaction of seeing Mackenzie King defeated in Prince Albert—albeit by a CCF candidate. The Prime Minister was returned in an Ontario by-election within months.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995155_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995155-53">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker staked out a position on the populist left of the PC party. Though most Canadians were content to look to Parliament for protection of <a href="/wiki/Civil_liberties" title="Civil liberties">civil liberties</a>, Diefenbaker called for a Bill of Rights, calling it "the only way to stop the march on the part of the government towards arbitrary power".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004194–195_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004194–195-46">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He objected to the great powers used by the Mackenzie King government to attempt to root out Soviet spies after the war, such as imprisonment without trial, and complained about the government's proclivity for letting its wartime powers become permanent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004194–195_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004194–195-46">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Leadership_contender_.281948.E2.80.931956.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership_contender_(1948–1956)">Leadership contender (1948–1956)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Leadership contender (1948–1956)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For further information on one of Diefenbaker's major legal cases during this time, see <a href="/wiki/Canoe_River_train_crash" title="Canoe River train crash">Canoe River train crash</a>.</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Johndiefenbaker.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Diefenbaker, standing in a legislative chamber, dramatically points in front of him. His hair is greying, and he appears much the way he will as Prime Minister." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Johndiefenbaker.jpg/220px-Johndiefenbaker.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Johndiefenbaker.jpg/330px-Johndiefenbaker.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Johndiefenbaker.jpg/440px-Johndiefenbaker.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="514" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker makes his point in the House of Commons, 1948.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In early 1948, Mackenzie King, now aged 73, announced his retirement; later that year <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a> succeeded him. Although Bracken had nearly doubled the Tory representation in the House, prominent Tories were increasingly unhappy with his leadership and pressured him to stand down. These party bosses believed that Ontario Premier <a href="/wiki/George_A._Drew" title="George A. Drew">George A. Drew</a>, who had won three successive provincial elections and had even made inroads in <a href="/wiki/Francophone_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Francophone Canadian">francophone</a> ridings, was the man to lead the Progressive Conservatives to victory. When Bracken resigned on July 17, 1948, Diefenbaker announced his candidacy. The party's backers, principally financiers headquartered on Toronto's <a href="/wiki/Bay_Street" title="Bay Street">Bay Street</a>, preferred Drew's conservative political stances to Diefenbaker's Western populism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995166–167_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995166–167-54">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Tory leaders packed the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1948" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1948">1948 leadership convention</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> in favour of Drew, appointing more than 300 delegates <a href="/wiki/At-large" title="At-large">at-large</a>. One cynical party member commented, "Ghost delegates with ghost ballots, marked by the ghostly hidden hand of Bay Street, are going to pick George Drew, and he'll deliver a ghost-written speech that'll cheer us all up, as we march briskly into a political graveyard."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196328_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196328-55">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Drew easily defeated Diefenbaker on the first ballot. St. Laurent called <a href="/wiki/1949_Canadian_federal_election" title="1949 Canadian federal election">an election for June 1949</a>, and the Tories were decimated, falling to 41 seats, only two more than the party's 1940 nadir.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30-56">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Despite intense efforts to make the Progressive Conservatives appeal to Quebecers, the party won only two seats in the province.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1975268–269_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1975268–269-57">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Newman argued that but for Diefenbaker's many defeats, he would never have become Prime Minister:
</p>
<blockquote><p>If, as a neophyte lawyer, he had succeeded in winning the Prince Albert seat in the federal elections of 1925 or 1926,&#160;... Diefenbaker would probably have been remembered only as an obscure minister in Bennett's Depression cabinet&#160;... If he had carried his home-town mayoralty in 1933,&#160;... he'd probably not be remembered at all&#160;... If he had succeeded in his bid for the national leadership in 1942, he might have taken the place of John Bracken on his six-year march to oblivion as leader of a party that had not changed itself enough to follow a Prairie radical&#160;... [If he had defeated Drew in 1948, he] would have been free to flounder before the political strength of Louis St. Laurent in the 1949 and 1953 campaigns.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman19635_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman19635-58">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The governing Liberals repeatedly attempted to draw Diefenbaker's seat out from under him. In 1948, Lake Centre was redistricted to remove areas which strongly supported Diefenbaker. In spite of that, he was returned in the 1949 election, the only PC member from Saskatchewan. In 1952, a redistricting committee dominated by Liberals abolished Lake Centre entirely, dividing its voters among three other ridings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30-56">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker stated in his memoirs that he had considered retiring from the House; with Drew only a year older than he was, the Westerner saw little prospect of advancement and had received tempting offers from Ontario law firms. However, the <a href="/wiki/Gerrymandering" title="Gerrymandering">gerrymandering</a> so angered him that he decided to fight for a seat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1975271–272_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1975271–272-59">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker's party had taken Prince Albert only once, in 1911, but he decided to stand in that riding for the <a href="/wiki/1953_Canadian_federal_election" title="1953 Canadian federal election">1953 election</a> and was successful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30_56-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30-56">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> He would hold that seat for the rest of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995573–574_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995573–574-60">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Even though Diefenbaker campaigned nationally for party candidates, the Progressive Conservatives gained little, rising to 51 seats as St. Laurent led the Liberals to a fifth successive majority.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995195_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995195-61">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> In addition to trying to secure his departure from Parliament, the government opened a home for unwed Indian mothers next door to <a href="/wiki/Diefenbaker_House" title="Diefenbaker House">Diefenbaker's home</a> in Prince Albert.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30_56-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196329–30-56">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker continued practising law. In 1951, he gained national attention by accepting the <i>Atherton</i> case, in which a young telegraph operator had been accused of negligently causing <a href="/wiki/Canoe_River_train_crash" title="Canoe River train crash">a train crash</a> by omitting crucial information from a message. Twenty-one people were killed, mostly Canadian troops bound for Korea. Diefenbaker paid $1,500 and sat a token <a href="/wiki/Bar_exam" class="mw-redirect" title="Bar exam">bar examination</a> to join the <a href="/wiki/Law_Society_of_British_Columbia" title="Law Society of British Columbia">Law Society of British Columbia</a> to take the case, and gained an acquittal, prejudicing the jury against the Crown prosecutor and pointing out a previous case in which interference had caused information to be lost in transmission.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995185–189_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995185–189-62">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the mid-1940s Edna began to suffer mental illness and was placed in a private psychiatric hospital for a time. She later fell ill from <a href="/wiki/Leukemia" title="Leukemia">leukemia</a> and died in 1951. In 1953, Diefenbaker married <a href="/wiki/Olive_Freeman" class="mw-redirect" title="Olive Freeman">Olive Palmer</a> (formerly Olive Freeman), whom he had courted while living in Wakaw. Olive Diefenbaker became a great source of strength to her husband. There were no children born of either marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004202_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004202-63">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> In 2013, claims were made that he fathered at least two sons out of wedlock, based on DNA testing showing a relationship between the two individuals, and that Diefenbaker employed both mothers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerkel2013_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerkel2013-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker won Prince Albert in 1953, even as the Tories suffered a second consecutive disastrous defeat under Drew. Speculation arose in the press that the leader might be pressured to step aside. Drew was determined to remain, however, and Diefenbaker was careful to avoid any action that might be seen as disloyal. However, Diefenbaker was never a member of the "Five O'clock Club" of Drew intimates who met the leader in his office for a drink and gossip each day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995199_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995199-65">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> By 1955, there was a widespread feeling among Tories that Drew was not capable of leading the party to a victory. At the same time, the Liberals were in flux as the aging St. Laurent tired of politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995200_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995200-66">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Drew was able to damage the government in a weeks-long battle over the <a href="/wiki/TransCanada_pipeline" title="TransCanada pipeline">TransCanada pipeline</a> in 1956—the so-called <a href="/wiki/Pipeline_Debate" title="Pipeline Debate">Pipeline Debate</a>—in which the government, in a hurry to obtain financing for the pipeline, imposed <a href="/wiki/Cloture" title="Cloture">closure</a> before the debate even began. The Tories and the CCF combined to obstruct business in the House for weeks before the Liberals were finally able to pass the measure. Diefenbaker played a relatively minor role in the Pipeline Debate, speaking only once.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995201–203_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995201–203-67">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<h3><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition.3B_1957_election"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition;_1957_election">Leader of the Opposition; 1957 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition; 1957 election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>By 1956, the <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit_Party_of_Canada" title="Social Credit Party of Canada">Social Credit Party</a> was becoming a potential rival to the Tories as Canada's main right-wing party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeisel196217–18_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeisel196217–18-68">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Canadian journalist and author <a href="/wiki/Bruce_Hutchison" title="Bruce Hutchison">Bruce Hutchison</a> discussed the state of the Tories in 1956:
</p>
<blockquote><p>When a party calling itself Conservative can think of nothing better than to outbid the Government's election promises; when it demands economy in one breath and increased spending in the next; when it proposes an immediate tax cut regardless of inflationary results&#160;... when in short, the Conservative party no longer gives us a conservative alternative after twenty-one years&#160;... then our political system desperately requires an opposition prepared to stand for something more than the improbable chance of quick victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeisel196216_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeisel196216-69">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In August 1956, Drew fell ill and many within the party urged him to step aside, feeling that the Progressive Conservatives needed vigorous leadership with an election likely within a year. He resigned in late September, and Diefenbaker immediately announced his candidacy for the leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004188_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004188-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> A number of Progressive Conservative leaders, principally from the Ontario wing of the party, started a "Stop Diefenbaker" movement, and wooed <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a> president <a href="/wiki/Sidney_Earle_Smith" title="Sidney Earle Smith">Sidney Smith</a> as a possible candidate. When Smith declined,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995203–204_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995203–204-71">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> they could find no one of comparable stature to stand against Diefenbaker. The only serious competition to Diefenbaker came from <a href="/wiki/Donald_Fleming" title="Donald Fleming">Donald Fleming</a>, who had finished third at the previous leadership convention, but his having repeatedly criticised Drew's leadership ensured that the critical Ontario delegates would not back Fleming, all but destroying his chances of victory. At the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1956" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1956">leadership convention in Ottawa in December 1956</a>, Diefenbaker won on the first ballot, and the dissidents reconciled themselves to his victory. After all, they reasoned, Diefenbaker was now 61 and unlikely to lead the party for more than one general election, an election they believed would be won by the Liberals regardless of who led the Tories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004188_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004188-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1957, Diefenbaker took his place as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">Leader of the Official Opposition</a>. In February, St. Laurent informed him that Parliament would be dissolved in April for an <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">election on June 10</a>. The Liberals submitted a budget in March; Diefenbaker attacked it for overly high taxes, failure to assist pensioners, and a lack of aid for the poorer provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995217–218_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995217–218-72">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Parliament was dissolved on April 12.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995224_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995224-73">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent was so confident of victory that he did not even bother to make recommendations to the Governor General to fill the 16 vacancies in the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992185_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992185-74">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196353_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196353-75">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker ran on a platform which concentrated on changes in domestic policies. He pledged to work with the provinces to reform the Senate. He proposed a vigorous new agricultural policy, seeking to stabilize income for farmers. He sought to reduce dependence on trade with the United States, and to seek closer ties with the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196352_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196352-76">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent called the Tory platform "a mere cream-puff of a thing—with more air than substance".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeisel1962158_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeisel1962158-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker and the PC party used television adroitly, whereas St. Laurent stated that he was more interested in seeing people than in talking to cameras.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196354_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196354-78">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Though the Liberals outspent the Progressive Conservatives three to one, according to Newman, their campaign had little imagination, and was based on telling voters that their only real option was to re-elect St. Laurent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196353_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196353-75">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker characterized the Tory program in a nationwide telecast on April 30:
</p>
<blockquote><p>It is a program&#160;... for a united Canada, for one Canada, for Canada first, in every aspect of our political and public life, for the welfare of the average man and woman. That is my approach to public affairs and has been throughout my life&#160;... A Canada, united from Coast to Coast, wherein there will be freedom for the individual, freedom of enterprise and where there will be a Government which, in all its actions, will remain the servant and not the master of the people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeisel1962286_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeisel1962286-79">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The final Gallup poll before the election showed the Liberals ahead, 48% to 34%.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995235_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995235-80">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Just before the election, <i><a href="/wiki/Maclean%27s" title="Maclean&#39;s">Maclean's</a></i> magazine printed its regular weekly issue, to go on sale the morning after the vote, editorializing that democracy in Canada was still strong despite a sixth consecutive Liberal victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196357–58_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196357–58-81">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> On election night, the Progressive Conservative advance started early, with the gain of two seats in reliably Liberal <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Newfoundland and Labrador">Newfoundland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196356_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196356-82">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The party picked up nine seats in <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a>, five in Quebec, 28 in Ontario, and at least one seat in every other province. The Progressive Conservatives took 112 seats to the Liberals' 105: a plurality, but not a majority.<sup id="cite_ref-1957_seats_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1957_seats-83">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> While the Liberals finished some 200,000 votes ahead of the Tories nationally, that margin was mostly wasted in overwhelming victories in safe Quebec seats. St. Laurent could have attempted to form a government, however, with the minor parties pledging to cooperate with the Progressive Conservatives, he would have likely faced a quick defeat at the Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196358_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196358-84">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> St. Laurent instead resigned, making Diefenbaker prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995238–240_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995238–240-85">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281957.E2.80.931963.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1957–1963)">Prime Minister (1957–1963)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1957–1963)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_events_and_policies">Domestic events and policies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Domestic events and policies"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Minority_government">Minority government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Minority government"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg/220px-Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="222" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg/330px-Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg/440px-Queen_Elizabeth_II_greeted_by_Prime_Minister_John_Diefenbaker.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1009" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker greeting Queen Elizabeth II, 1957</figcaption></figure>
<p>When John Diefenbaker took office as Prime Minister of Canada on June 21, 1957, only one Progressive Conservative MP, <a href="/wiki/William_Earl_Rowe" title="William Earl Rowe">Earl Rowe</a>, had served in federal governmental office, for a brief period under Bennett in 1935. Rowe was no friend of Diefenbaker — he had briefly served as the party's acting leader in-between Drew's resignation and Diefenbaker's election, and did not definitively rule himself out of running to succeed Drew permanently until a relatively late stage, contributing to Diefenbaker's mistrust of him — and was given no place in his government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995244_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995244-86">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker appointed <a href="/wiki/Ellen_Fairclough" title="Ellen Fairclough">Ellen Fairclough</a> as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Canada" title="Secretary of State for Canada">Secretary of State for Canada</a>, the first woman to be appointed to a Cabinet post, and <a href="/wiki/Michael_Starr_(politician)" title="Michael Starr (politician)">Michael Starr</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Labour_(Canada)" title="Minister of Labour (Canada)">Minister of Labour</a>, the first <a href="/wiki/Ukrainian_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Ukrainian Canadian">Canadian of Ukrainian descent</a> to serve in Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDaniell1957_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaniell1957-87">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As <a href="/wiki/Centre_Block" title="Centre Block">the Parliament buildings</a> had been lent to the <a href="/wiki/Universal_Postal_Union" title="Universal Postal Union">Universal Postal Union</a> for its 14th congress, Diefenbaker was forced to wait until the fall to convene Parliament. However, the Cabinet approved measures that summer, including increased price supports for butter and turkeys, and raises for federal employees.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196361–63_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196361–63-88">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Once the <a href="/wiki/23rd_Canadian_Parliament" title="23rd Canadian Parliament">23rd Canadian Parliament</a> was opened on October 14 by <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth II</a> – the first to be opened by any <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada" title="Monarchy of Canada">Canadian monarch</a> – the government rapidly passed legislation, including tax cuts and increases in old age pensions. The Liberals were ineffective in opposition, with the party in the midst of a leadership race after St. Laurent's resignation as party leader.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman196363–65_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman196363–65-89">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the Conservatives leading in the polls, Diefenbaker wanted a new election, hopeful that his party would gain a majority of seats. The strong Liberal presence meant that the Governor General could refuse a dissolution request early in a parliament's term and allow them to form government if Diefenbaker resigned.  Diefenbaker sought a pretext for a new election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995272–273_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995272–273-90">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Such an excuse presented itself when former Secretary of State for External Affairs <a href="/wiki/Lester_Pearson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester Pearson">Lester Pearson</a> attended his first parliamentary session as Leader of the Opposition on January 20, 1958, four days after becoming the Liberal leader. In his first speech as leader, Pearson (recently returned from <a href="/wiki/Oslo" title="Oslo">Oslo</a> where he had been awarded the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a>), moved an amendment to <a href="/wiki/Loss_of_supply" title="Loss of supply">supply</a>, and called, not for an election, but for the Progressive Conservatives to resign, allowing the Liberals to form a government. Pearson stated that the condition of the economy required "a Government pledged to implement Liberal policies".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992200_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992200-91">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Government MPs laughed at Pearson, as did members of the press who were present. Pearson later recorded in his memoirs that he knew that his "first attack on the government had been a failure, indeed a fiasco".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992200_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992200-91">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker spoke for two hours and three minutes, and devastated his Liberal opposition. He mocked Pearson, contrasting the party leader's address at the Liberal leadership convention with his speech to the House:
</p>
<blockquote><p>On Thursday there was shrieking defiance, on the following Monday there is shrinking indecision&#160;... The only reason that this motion is worded as it is[,] is that my honourable friends opposite quake when they think of what will happen if an election comes&#160;... It is the resignation from responsibility of a great party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995276_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995276-92">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Diefenbaker read from an internal report provided to the St. Laurent government in early 1957, warning that a recession was coming, and stated:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Across the way, Mr. Speaker, sit the purveyors of gloom who would endeavour for political purposes, to panic the Canadian people&#160;... They had a warning&#160;... Did they tell us that? No. Mr. Speaker, why did they not reveal this? Why did they not act when the House was sitting in January, February, March, and April? They had the information&#160;... You concealed the facts, that is what you did.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995287–288_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995287–288-93">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>According to the Minister of Finance, <a href="/wiki/Donald_Fleming" title="Donald Fleming">Donald Fleming</a>, "Pearson looked at first merry, then serious, then uncomfortable, then disturbed, and finally sick."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995276_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995276-92">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Pearson recorded in his memoirs that the Prime Minister "tore me to shreds".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992200_91-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992200-91">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Prominent Liberal frontbencher <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin_Sr." title="Paul Martin Sr.">Paul Martin</a> called Diefenbaker's response "one of the greatest devastating speeches" and "Diefenbaker's great hour".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197588_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197588-94">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> On February 1, Diefenbaker asked the Governor General, <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Massey" title="Vincent Massey">Vincent Massey</a>, to dissolve Parliament, alleging that though St. Laurent had promised cooperation, Pearson had made it clear he would not follow his predecessor's lead. Massey agreed to the dissolution, and Diefenbaker set an election date of March 31, 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197589_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197589-95">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995278_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995278-96">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1958_election">1958 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: 1958 election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">1958 election</a> campaign saw a huge outpouring of public support for the Progressive Conservatives. At the opening campaign rally in <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a> on February 12 voters filled the hall until the doors had to be closed for safety reasons. They were promptly broken down by the crowd outside.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995279_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995279-97">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> At the rally, Diefenbaker called for "[a] new vision. A new hope. A new soul for Canada."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199049_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199049-98">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> He pledged to open the Canadian North, to seek out its resources and make it a place for settlements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995279_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995279-97">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> The conclusion to his speech expounded on what became known as "The Vision",
</p>
<blockquote><p>This is the vision: One Canada. One Canada, where Canadians will have preserved to them the control of their own economic and political destiny. Sir <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a> saw a Canada from east to west: he opened the west. I see a new Canada—a Canada of the North.
