This is the vision!<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995280_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995280-99">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Pierre_S%C3%A9vigny_(politician)" title="Pierre Sévigny (politician)">Pierre Sévigny</a>, who would be elected an MP in 1958, recalled the gathering, "When he had finished that speech, as he was walking to the door, I saw people kneel and kiss his coat. Not one, but many. People were in tears. People were delirious. And this happened many a time after."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197598_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197598-100">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> When Sévigny introduced Diefenbaker to a Montreal rally with the words <i>"Levez-vous, levez-vous, saluez votre chef!"</i> (Rise, rise, salute your chief!) according to <a href="/wiki/Postmaster_General_of_Canada" title="Postmaster General of Canada">Postmaster General</a> <a href="/wiki/William_McLean_Hamilton" title="William McLean Hamilton">William Hamilton</a> "thousands and thousands of people, jammed into that auditorium, just tore the roof off in a frenzy."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197594_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197594-101">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Michael Starr remembered, "That was the most fantastic election&#160;... I went into little places. <a href="/wiki/Smoky_Lake,_Alberta" class="mw-redirect" title="Smoky Lake, Alberta">Smoky Lake, Alberta</a>, where nobody ever saw a minister. <a href="/wiki/Canora,_Saskatchewan" title="Canora, Saskatchewan">Canora, Saskatchewan</a>. Every meeting was jammed&#160;... The halls would be filled with people and sitting there in the front would be the first Ukrainian immigrants with shawls and hands gnarled from work&#160;... I would switch to Ukrainian and the tears would start to run down their faces&#160;... I don't care who says what won the election; it was the emotional aspect that really caught on."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197595_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197595-102">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pearson and his Liberals faltered badly in the campaign. The Liberal Party leader tried to make an issue of the fact that Diefenbaker had called a winter election, generally disfavoured in Canada due to travel difficulties. Pearson's objection cut little ice with voters, and served only to remind the electorate that the Liberals, at their convention, had called for an election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992201–202_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992201–202-103">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Pearson mocked Diefenbaker's northern plans as "igloo-to-igloo" communications, and was assailed by the Prime Minister for being condescending.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992203_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992203-104">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal leader spoke to small, quiet crowds, which quickly left the halls when he was done.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992201–202_103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992201–202-103">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> By election day, Pearson had no illusions that he might win the election, and hoped only to salvage 100 seats. The Liberals would be limited to less than half of that.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992201–202_103-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1992201–202-103">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On March 31, 1958, the Tories won what is still the largest majority (in terms of percentage of seats) in Canadian federal political history, winning 208 seats to the Liberals' 48, with the CCF winning 8 and Social Credit wiped out. The Progressive Conservatives won a majority of the votes and of the seats in every province except British Columbia (49.8%) and Newfoundland. Quebec's <i><a href="/wiki/Union_Nationale_(Quebec)" title="Union Nationale (Quebec)">Union Nationale</a></i> political machine had given the PC party little support, but with Quebec voters minded to support Diefenbaker, <i>Union Nationale</i> boss <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Duplessis" title="Maurice Duplessis">Maurice Duplessis</a> threw the machinery of his party behind the Tories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995282_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995282-105">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Mandate_.281958.E2.80.931962.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mandate_(1958–1962)">Mandate (1958–1962)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Mandate (1958–1962)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg/220px-John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg/330px-John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg/440px-John_Diefenbaker_and_Charles_de_Gaulle_1958.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="453" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker with Charles de Gaulle, 1958</figcaption></figure>
<p>An economic downturn was beginning in Canada by 1958. Because of tax cuts instituted the previous year, the budget presented by the government predicted a small deficit for 1957–58, and a large one, $648&#160;million, for the following year. Minister of Finance Fleming and <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Canada" title="Bank of Canada">Bank of Canada</a> Governor <a href="/wiki/James_Elliott_Coyne" title="James Elliott Coyne">James Coyne</a> proposed that the wartime <a href="/wiki/War_bond#Canada_in_World_War_II" title="War bond">Victory Bond</a> issue, which constituted two-thirds of the national debt and which was due to be redeemed by 1967, be refinanced to a longer term. After considerable indecision on Diefenbaker's part, a nationwide campaign took place, and 90% of the bonds were converted. However, this transaction led to an increase in the <a href="/wiki/Money_supply" title="Money supply">money supply</a>, which in future years would hamper the government's efforts to respond to unemployment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995287–289_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995287–289-106">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a trial lawyer, and in opposition, Diefenbaker had long been concerned with civil liberties. On July 1, 1960, <a href="/wiki/Canada_Day" title="Canada Day">Dominion Day</a>, he introduced the <i>Canadian Bill of Rights</i> in Parliament, and the bill rapidly passed and was proclaimed on August 10, fulfilling a lifetime goal of Diefenbaker's, having begun drafting it as early as 1936.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995335_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995335-107">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The document purported to guarantee fundamental freedoms, with special attention to the rights of the accused. However, as a mere piece of federal legislation, it could be amended by any other law, and the question of civil liberties was to a large extent a provincial matter, outside of federal jurisdiction. One lawyer remarked that the document provided rights for all Canadians, "so long as they don't live in any of the provinces".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004195–196_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004195–196-109">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker had appointed the first First Nations member of the Senate, <a href="/wiki/James_Gladstone" title="James Gladstone">James Gladstone</a> in January 1958,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman1963101_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman1963101-110">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> and in 1960, his government extended voting rights to all native people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In 1962, Diefenbaker's government eliminated race discrimination clauses in immigration laws.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker pursued a "One Canada" policy, seeking equality of all Canadians. As part of that philosophy, he was unwilling to make special concessions to Quebec's francophones. Thomas Van Dusen, who served as Diefenbaker's executive assistant and wrote a book about him, characterized the leader's views on this issue:
</p>
<blockquote><p>There must be no compromise with Canada's existence as a nation. Opting out, two flags, two pension plans, associated states, Two Nations and all the other baggage of political dualism was ushering Quebec out of Confederation on the instalment plan. He could not accept any theory of two nations, however worded, because it would make of those neither French nor English second-class citizens.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Dusen196879_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Dusen196879-112">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Diefenbaker's disinclination to make concessions to Quebec, along with the disintegration of the <i>Union Nationale</i>, the failure of the Tories to build an effective structure in Quebec, and Diefenbaker appointing few Quebecers to his Cabinet, none to senior positions, all led to an erosion of Progressive Conservative support in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995284,_367,_414_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995284,_367,_414-113">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker did recommend the appointment of the first French-Canadian governor general, <a href="/wiki/Georges_Vanier" title="Georges Vanier">Georges Vanier</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diefendollars.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Diefendollars.jpeg/220px-Diefendollars.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="315" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Diefendollars.jpeg/330px-Diefendollars.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Diefendollars.jpeg/440px-Diefendollars.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="2666" data-file-height="3819" /></a><figcaption>Three "Diefendollars", used to mock Diefenbaker during the 1962 campaign.</figcaption></figure>
<p>By mid-1961, differences in <a href="/wiki/Monetary_policy" title="Monetary policy">monetary policy</a> led to open conflict with Bank of Canada Governor Coyne, who adhered to a tight money policy. Appointed by St. Laurent to a term expiring in December 1961, Coyne could only be dismissed before then by the passing of an Act of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995393–394_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995393–394-114">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Coyne defended his position by giving public speeches, to the dismay of the government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995397–399_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995397–399-115">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> The Cabinet was also angered when it learned that Coyne and his board had passed amendments to the bank's pension scheme which greatly increased Coyne's pension, without publishing the amendments in the <i><a href="/wiki/Canada_Gazette" title="Canada Gazette">Canada Gazette</a></i> as required by law. Negotiations between Minister of Finance Fleming and Coyne for the latter's resignation broke down, with the governor making the dispute public, and Diefenbaker sought to dismiss Coyne by legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995400–406_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995400–406-116">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker was able to get legislation to dismiss Coyne through the House, but the Liberal-controlled Senate invited Coyne to testify before one of its committees. After giving the governor a platform against the government, the committee then chose to take no further action, adding its view that Coyne had done nothing wrong. Once he had the opportunity to testify (denied him in the Commons), Coyne resigned, keeping his increased pension, and the government was extensively criticized in the press.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995412–413_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995412–413-117">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the time Diefenbaker called an <a href="/wiki/1962_Canadian_federal_election" title="1962 Canadian federal election">election for June 18, 1962</a>, the party had been damaged by loss of support in Quebec and in urban areas<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995442_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995442-118">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> as voters grew disillusioned with Diefenbaker and the Tories. The PC campaign was hurt when the Bank of Canada was forced to devalue the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_dollar" title="Canadian dollar">Canadian dollar</a> to <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1154941027">.mw-parser-output .frac{white-space:nowrap}.mw-parser-output .frac .num,.mw-parser-output .frac .den{font-size:80%;line-height:0;vertical-align:super}.mw-parser-output .frac .den{vertical-align:sub}.mw-parser-output .sr-only{border:0;clip:rect(0,0,0,0);clip-path:polygon(0px 0px,0px 0px,0px 0px);height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;padding:0;position:absolute;width:1px}</style><span class="frac">92<span class="sr-only">+</span><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span>&#160;US cents; it had previously hovered in the range from 95&#160;cents to par with the United States dollar. Privately printed satirical "Diefenbucks" swept the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995437–439_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995437–439-119">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> On election day, the Progressive Conservatives lost 92 seats, but were still able to form a minority government. The New Democratic Party (the successor to the CCF) and Social Credit held the balance of power in the new Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995442_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995442-118">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Foreign policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Britain_and_the_Commonwealth">Britain and the Commonwealth</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Britain and the Commonwealth"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations,_at_Windsor_Castle_(1960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A number of older men in formal dress surround a young woman with a white gown." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations%2C_at_Windsor_Castle_%281960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference%29.jpg/220px-Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations%2C_at_Windsor_Castle_%281960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations%2C_at_Windsor_Castle_%281960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference%29.jpg/330px-Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations%2C_at_Windsor_Castle_%281960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations%2C_at_Windsor_Castle_%281960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference%29.jpg/440px-Queen_Elizabeth_II_and_the_Prime_Ministers_of_the_Commonwealth_Nations%2C_at_Windsor_Castle_%281960_Commonwealth_Prime_Minister%27s_Conference%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2200" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker stands to the right of Queen <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a> at the 1960 Commonwealth Prime Ministers' Conference.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Diefenbaker attended a <a href="/wiki/1957_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%27_Conference" title="1957 Commonwealth Prime Ministers&#39; Conference">meeting of the Commonwealth Prime Ministers</a> in London shortly after taking office in 1957. He generated headlines by proposing that 15% of Canadian spending on US imports instead be spent on imports from the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995251–53_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995251–53-120">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Britain responded with an offer of a free trade agreement, which was rejected by the Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995255–56_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995255–56-121">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> As the <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> government in the UK sought to enter the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Community" title="European Economic Community">Common Market</a>, Diefenbaker feared that Canadian exports to the UK would be threatened. He also believed that the mother country should place the Commonwealth first, and sought to discourage Britain's entry. The British were annoyed at Canadian interference. Britain's initial attempt to enter the Common Market was vetoed by French President <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman1963272–274_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman1963272–274-122">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Through 1959, the Diefenbaker government had a policy of not criticizing South Africa and its <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a> government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGabriel198753_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabriel198753-123">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> In this stance, Diefenbaker had the support of the Liberals but not that of CCF leader <a href="/wiki/Hazen_Argue" title="Hazen Argue">Hazen Argue</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGabriel198756–57_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabriel198756–57-124">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> In 1960, however, the South Africans sought to maintain membership in the Commonwealth even if South African white voters chose to make the country a republic in a <a href="/wiki/1960_South_African_republic_referendum" title="1960 South African republic referendum">referendum scheduled for later that year</a>. South Africa asked that year's <a href="/wiki/1960_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%27_Conference" title="1960 Commonwealth Prime Ministers&#39; Conference">Commonwealth Prime Ministers' Conference</a> to allow it to remain in the Commonwealth regardless of the result of the referendum. Diefenbaker privately expressed his distaste for apartheid to South African External Affairs Minister <a href="/wiki/Eric_Louw" title="Eric Louw">Eric Louw</a> and urged him to give the black and coloured people of South Africa at least the minimal representation they had originally had. Louw, attending the conference as Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Hendrik_Verwoerd" title="Hendrik Verwoerd">Hendrik Verwoerd</a> recovered from an assassination attempt, refused.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGabriel198758–63_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabriel198758–63-125">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> The conference resolved that an advance decision would be interfering in South Africa's internal affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1976211–212_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1976211–212-126">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On October 5, 1960, South Africa's white voters decided to make the country a republic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGabriel198766_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabriel198766-127">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/1961_Commonwealth_Prime_Ministers%27_Conference" title="1961 Commonwealth Prime Ministers&#39; Conference">Prime Ministers' Conference</a> in 1961, Verwoerd formally applied for South Africa to remain in the Commonwealth. The prime ministers were divided; Diefenbaker broke the deadlock by proposing that South Africa only be re-admitted if it joined other states in condemning apartheid in principle. Once it became clear that South Africa's membership would be rejected, Verwoerd withdrew his country's application to remain in the Commonwealth and left the group. According to Peter Newman, this was "Diefenbaker's most important contribution to international politics&#160;... Diefenbaker flew home, a hero."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman1963258_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman1963258-128">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Policy_towards_the_United_States">Policy towards the United States</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Policy towards the United States"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Ike_and_John:_the_Eisenhower_years">Ike and John: the Eisenhower years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Ike and John: the Eisenhower years"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_(January_1961).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Diefenbaker and a Dwight Eisenhower sit at a table. Two women and three men stand behind them." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_%28January_1961%29.jpg/220px-John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_%28January_1961%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_%28January_1961%29.jpg/330px-John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_%28January_1961%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_%28January_1961%29.jpg/440px-John_Diefenbaker_and_Dwight_Eisenhower_at_signing_of_Columbia_River_Treaty_%28January_1961%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2156" data-file-height="1698" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker (seated left) and US President <a href="/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower" title="Dwight D. Eisenhower">Dwight D. Eisenhower</a> at the signing of the Columbia River Treaty, 1961.</figcaption></figure>
<p>American officials were uncomfortable with Diefenbaker's initial election, believing they had heard undertones of anti-Americanism in the campaign. After years of the Liberals, one US State Department official noted, "We'll be dealing with an unknown quantity."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199046_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199046-129">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> US officials viewed Diefenbaker's 1958 landslide with disappointment; they knew and liked Pearson from his years in diplomacy and felt the Liberal Party leader would be more likely to institute pro-American policies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199050_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199050-130">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> However, US President <a href="/wiki/Dwight_Eisenhower" class="mw-redirect" title="Dwight Eisenhower">Dwight Eisenhower</a> took pains to foster good relations with Diefenbaker. The two men found much in common, from Western farm backgrounds to a love of fishing, and Diefenbaker had an admiration for war leaders such as Eisenhower and <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Churchill</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199054–55_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199054–55-131">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker wrote in his memoirs, "I might add that President Eisenhower and I were from our first meeting on an 'Ike–John' basis, and that we were as close as the nearest telephone."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1976157_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1976157-132">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> The Eisenhower–Diefenbaker relationship was sufficiently strong that the touchy Canadian Prime Minister was prepared to overlook slights. When Eisenhower addressed Parliament in October 1958, he downplayed trade concerns that Diefenbaker had publicly expressed. Diefenbaker said nothing and took Eisenhower fishing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199056–57_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199056–57-133">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker had approved plans to join the United States in what became known as <a href="/wiki/NORAD" title="NORAD">NORAD</a>, an integrated air defence system, in mid-1957.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995292_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995292-134">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Despite Liberal misgivings that Diefenbaker had committed Canada to the system before consulting either the Cabinet or Parliament, Pearson and his followers voted with the government to approve NORAD in June 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995295–296_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995295–296-135">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1959, the Diefenbaker government cancelled the development and manufacture of the <a href="/wiki/CF-105_Arrow" class="mw-redirect" title="CF-105 Arrow">Avro CF-105 Arrow</a>. The Arrow was a supersonic jet interceptor built by <a href="/wiki/Avro_Canada" title="Avro Canada">Avro Canada</a> in <a href="/wiki/Malton,_Ontario" class="mw-redirect" title="Malton, Ontario">Malton, Ontario</a>, to defend Canada in the event of a <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet</a> attack. The interceptor had been under development since 1953, and had suffered from many cost overruns and complications.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995307–308_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995307–308-136">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> In 1955, the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force" title="Royal Canadian Air Force">RCAF</a> stated it would need only nine squadrons of Arrows, down from 20, as originally proposed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995307–308_136-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995307–308-136">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/C._D._Howe" title="C. D. Howe">C. D. Howe</a>, the former minister responsible for postwar reconstruction, the St. Laurent government had serious misgivings about continuing the Arrow program, and planned to discuss its termination after the 1957 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStewart1991254–255_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart1991254–255-137">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> In the run-up to the 1958 election, with three Tory-held seats at risk in the Malton area, the Diefenbaker government authorized further funding.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995309_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995309-138">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Even though the first test flights of the Arrow were successful, the US government was unwilling to commit to a purchase of aircraft from Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995310_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995310-139">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> In September 1958, Diefenbaker warned<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995316_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995316-140">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> that the Arrow would come under complete review in six months.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStewart1991244–245_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart1991244–245-141">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> The company began seeking out other projects including a US-funded "saucer" program that became the <a href="/wiki/VZ-9_AV_Avrocar" class="mw-redirect" title="VZ-9 AV Avrocar">VZ-9 Avrocar</a>, and also mounted a public relations offensive urging that the Arrow go into full production.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995317_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995317-142">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> On February 20, 1959, the Cabinet decided to cancel the Avro Arrow, following an earlier decision to permit the United States to build two <a href="/wiki/Bomarc" class="mw-redirect" title="Bomarc">Bomarc</a> missile bases in Canada. The company immediately dismissed its 14,000 employees, blaming Diefenbaker for the firings, though it rehired 2,500 employees to fulfil existing obligations.<sup id="cite_ref-Avro_jobs_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Avro_jobs-143">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although the two leaders had a strong relationship, by 1960 US officials were becoming concerned by what they viewed as Canadian procrastination on vital issues, such as whether Canada should join the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">Organization of American States</a> (OAS). Talks on these issues in June 1960 produced little in results.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199056–57_133-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199056–57-133">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker hoped that US Vice President <a href="/wiki/Richard_Nixon" title="Richard Nixon">Richard Nixon</a> would win the <a href="/wiki/1960_United_States_presidential_election" title="1960 United States presidential election">1960 US presidential election</a>, but when Nixon's Democratic rival, Senator <a href="/wiki/John_F._Kennedy" title="John F. Kennedy">John F. Kennedy</a> won the race, he sent Senator Kennedy a note of congratulations. Kennedy did not respond until Canadian officials asked what had become of Diefenbaker's note, two weeks later. Diefenbaker, for whom such correspondence was very meaningful, was annoyed at the <a href="/wiki/President-elect_of_the_United_States" title="President-elect of the United States">President-elect</a>'s slowness to respond.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199059–61_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199059–61-144">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> In January 1961, Diefenbaker visited Washington to sign the <a href="/wiki/Columbia_River_Treaty" title="Columbia River Treaty">Columbia River Treaty</a>. However, with only days remaining in the Eisenhower administration, little else could be accomplished.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash199061–62_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash199061–62-145">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Bilateral_antipathy:_the_Kennedy_administration">Bilateral antipathy: the Kennedy administration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Bilateral antipathy: the Kennedy administration"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia,_Newfoundland_(17_May_1962).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Diefenbaker, wearing a coat over his suit, shakes hands with a smiling man. Two other men, looking less impressed, are in the background." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia%2C_Newfoundland_%2817_May_1962%29.jpg/220px-John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia%2C_Newfoundland_%2817_May_1962%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia%2C_Newfoundland_%2817_May_1962%29.jpg/330px-John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia%2C_Newfoundland_%2817_May_1962%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia%2C_Newfoundland_%2817_May_1962%29.jpg/440px-John_Diefenbaker_at_the_US_Naval_base_at_Argentia%2C_Newfoundland_%2817_May_1962%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2874" data-file-height="2265" /></a><figcaption>Diefenbaker (front right) at the US Naval base in Argentia, Newfoundland during the 1962 campaign.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kennedy and Diefenbaker started off well but matters soon worsened. When the two met in Washington on February 20, Diefenbaker was impressed by Kennedy, and invited him to visit Ottawa.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>  Kennedy, however, told his aides that he never wanted "to see the boring son of a bitch again".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995382_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995382-147">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> The Ottawa visit began awkwardly.  Kennedy accidentally left behind a briefing note suggesting he "push" Diefenbaker on several issues, including the decision to accept nuclear weapons on Canadian soil, which bitterly divided the Canadian Cabinet. Diefenbaker was also annoyed by Kennedy's speech to Parliament, in which he urged Canada to join the OAS (which Diefenbaker had already rejected),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995385–388_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995385–388-148">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> and by the President spending most of his time talking to Leader of the Opposition Pearson at the formal dinner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990126–128_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990126–128-149">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Montreal_Gazette&#39;&#39;_1979-08-17,_Obituary_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Montreal_Gazette&#39;&#39;_1979-08-17,_Obituary-150">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Both Kennedy and <a href="/wiki/Jacqueline_Kennedy" class="mw-redirect" title="Jacqueline Kennedy">his wife Jackie</a> were bored by Diefenbaker's Churchill anecdotes at lunch, stories that Jackie Kennedy later described as "painful".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDruzin2011_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDruzin2011-151">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker was initially inclined to go along with Kennedy's request that nuclear weapons be stationed on Canadian soil as part of NORAD. However, when an August 3, 1961, letter from Kennedy which urged this was leaked to the media, Diefenbaker was angered and withdrew his support. The Prime Minister was also influenced by a massive <a href="/wiki/Anti-nuclear_movement_in_Canada" title="Anti-nuclear movement in Canada">demonstration against nuclear weapons</a>, which took place on <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a>. Diefenbaker was handed a petition containing 142,000 names.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990139–141_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990139–141-152">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1962, the American government was becoming increasingly concerned at the lack of a commitment from Canada to take nuclear weapons. The interceptors and Bomarc missiles with which Canada was being supplied as a NORAD member were either of no use or of greatly diminished utility without nuclear devices.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990144_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990144-153">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Canadian and American military officers launched a quiet campaign to make this known to the press, and to advocate Canadian agreement to acquire the warheads.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990144–146_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990144–146-154">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker was also upset when Pearson was invited to the White House for a dinner for Nobel Prize winners in April, and met with the President privately for 40 minutes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990156–158_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990156–158-155">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> When the Prime Minister met with retiring American Ambassador <a href="/wiki/Livingston_Merchant" class="mw-redirect" title="Livingston Merchant">Livingston Merchant</a>, he angrily disclosed the paper Kennedy had left behind, and hinted that he might make use of it in the upcoming election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995433–435_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995433–435-156">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Merchant's report caused consternation in Washington, and the ambassador was sent back to see Diefenbaker again. This time, he found Diefenbaker calm, and the Prime Minister pledged not to use the memo, and to give Merchant advance word if he changed his mind.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995435–436_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995435–436-157">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Canada appointed a new <a href="/wiki/Embassy_of_Canada_in_Washington,_D.C." class="mw-redirect" title="Embassy of Canada in Washington, D.C.">ambassador to Washington</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Ritchie_(diplomat)" title="Charles Ritchie (diplomat)">Charles Ritchie</a>, who on arrival received a cool reception from Kennedy and found that the squabble was affecting progress on a number of issues.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990162–164_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990162–164-158">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kennedy was careful to avoid overt favouritism during the <a href="/wiki/1962_Canadian_federal_election" title="1962 Canadian federal election">1962 Canadian election</a> campaign. Several times during the campaign, Diefenbaker stated that the Kennedy administration desired his defeat because he refused to "bow down to Washington".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990167–168_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990167–168-159">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> After Diefenbaker was returned with a minority, Washington continued to press for acceptance of nuclear arms, but Diefenbaker, faced with a split between Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Douglas_Harkness" title="Douglas Harkness">Douglas Harkness</a> and External Affairs Minister <a href="/wiki/Howard_Charles_Green" title="Howard Charles Green">Howard Green</a> on the question, continued to stall, hoping that time and events would invite consensus.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990176–177_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990176–177-160">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When the <a href="/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis" title="Cuban Missile Crisis">Cuban Missile Crisis</a> erupted in October 1962, Kennedy chose not to consult with Diefenbaker before making decisions on what actions to take. The US president sent former Ambassador Merchant to Ottawa to inform the Prime Minister as to the content of the speech that Kennedy was to make on television. Diefenbaker was upset at both the lack of consultation and the fact that he was given less than two hours advance word.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990180–184_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990180–184-161">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> He was angered again when the US government released a statement stating that it had Canada's full support.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990189–190_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990189–190-162">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> In a statement to the Commons, Diefenbaker proposed sending representatives of neutral nations to Cuba to verify the American allegations, which Washington took to mean that he was questioning Kennedy's word.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990188–189_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990188–189-163">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> When American forces went to a heightened alert, <a href="/wiki/DEFCON" title="DEFCON">DEFCON</a> 3, Diefenbaker was slow to order Canadian forces to match it. Harkness and the Chiefs of Staff had Canadian forces clandestinely go to that alert status anyway,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990194_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990194-164">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> and Diefenbaker eventually authorized it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990200_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990200-165">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> The crisis ended without war, and polls found that Kennedy's actions were widely supported by Canadians. Diefenbaker was severely criticized in the media.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990203–204_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990203–204-166">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Downfall">Downfall</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Downfall"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diefenbaker_button.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Button, &quot;Keep the Chief&quot;" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Diefenbaker_button.jpg/170px-Diefenbaker_button.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="168" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Diefenbaker_button.jpg/255px-Diefenbaker_button.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Diefenbaker_button.jpg/340px-Diefenbaker_button.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1871" data-file-height="1849" /></a><figcaption>Button urging Diefenbaker's re-election</figcaption></figure>
<p>On January 3, 1963, <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Allied_Commander_Europe" title="Supreme Allied Commander Europe">NATO Supreme Commander</a> General <a href="/wiki/Lauris_Norstad" title="Lauris Norstad">Lauris Norstad</a> visited Ottawa, in one of a series of visits to member nations prior to his retirement. At a news conference, Norstad stated that if Canada did not accept nuclear weapons, it would not be fulfilling its commitments to NATO. Newspapers across Canada criticized Diefenbaker, who was convinced the statement was part of a plot by Kennedy to bring down his government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990223–225_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990223–225-167">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Although the Liberals had been previously indecisive on the question of nuclear weapons, on January 12, Pearson made a speech stating that the government should live up to its commitments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995469_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995469-168">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the Cabinet still divided between adherents of Green and Harkness, Diefenbaker made a speech in the Commons on January 25 that Fleming (by then <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">Minister of Justice</a>) termed "a model of obfuscation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995471–472_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995471–472-169">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Harkness was initially convinced that Diefenbaker was saying that he would support nuclear warheads in Canada. After talking to the press, he realized that his view of the speech was not universally shared, and he asked Diefenbaker for clarification. Diefenbaker, however, continued to try to avoid taking a firm position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995471–472_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995471–472-169">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> On January 30, the US State Department issued a press release suggesting that Diefenbaker had made misstatements in his Commons speech. For the first time ever, Canada recalled its ambassador to Washington as a diplomatic protest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990245_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990245-170">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> Though all parties condemned the State Department action, the three parties outside the government demanded that Diefenbaker take a stand on the nuclear weapon issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995475_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995475-171">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The bitter divisions within the Cabinet continued, with Diefenbaker deliberating whether to call an election on the issue of American interference in Canadian politics. At least six Cabinet ministers favoured Diefenbaker's ouster. Finally, at a dramatic Cabinet meeting on Sunday, February 3, Harkness told Diefenbaker that the Prime Minister no longer had the confidence of the Canadian people, and resigned. Diefenbaker asked ministers supporting him to stand, and when only about half did, stated that he was going to see the Governor General to resign, and that Fleming would be the next Prime Minister. Green called his Cabinet colleagues a "nest of traitors", but eventually cooler heads prevailed, and the Prime Minister was urged to return and to fight the <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence" title="Motion of no confidence">motion of non-confidence</a> scheduled for the following day. Harkness, however, persisted in his resignation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995478–479_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995478–479-172">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Negotiations with the <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit_Party_of_Canada" title="Social Credit Party of Canada">Social Credit Party</a>, which had enough votes to save the government, failed, and the government fell, 142–111.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995485_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995485-173">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two members of the government resigned the day after the government lost the vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197680_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197680-174">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> As the campaign opened, the Tories trailed in the polls by 15 points. To Pearson and his Liberals, the only question was how large a majority they would win.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990273_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990273-175">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Peter Stursberg, who wrote two books about the Diefenbaker years, stated of that campaign:
</p>
<blockquote><p>For the old Diefenbaker was in full cry. All the agony of the disintegration of his government was gone, and he seemed to be a giant revived by his contact with the people. This was Diefenbaker's finest election. He was virtually alone on the hustings. Even such loyalists as <a href="/wiki/Gordon_Churchill" title="Gordon Churchill">Gordon Churchill</a> had to stick close to their own bailiwicks, where they were fighting for their political lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197689_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197689-176">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Though the White House maintained public neutrality, privately Kennedy made it clear he desired a Liberal victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995504_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995504-177">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> Kennedy lent Lou Harris, his pollster to work for the Liberals again.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg197694_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg197694-178">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> On <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">election day</a>, April 8, 1963, the Liberals claimed 129 seats to the Tories' 95, five seats short of an absolute majority. Diefenbaker held to power for several days, until six <a href="/wiki/Ralliement_cr%C3%A9ditiste#Ralliement_des_créditistes_du_Canada" title="Ralliement créditiste">Quebec Social Credit</a> MPs signed a statement that Pearson should form the government. These votes would be enough to give Pearson support of a majority of the House of Commons, and Diefenbaker resigned. The six MPs repudiated the statement within days. Nonetheless, Pearson formed a government with the support of the NDP.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995509–510_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995509–510-179">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_years_.281963.E2.80.931979.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_years_(1963–1979)">Later years (1963–1979)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Later years (1963–1979)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_opposition">Return to opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Return to opposition"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Diefenbaker continued to lead the Progressive Conservatives, again as Leader of the Opposition. In November 1963, upon hearing of <a href="/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_assassination" class="mw-redirect" title="John F. Kennedy assassination">Kennedy's assassination</a>, the Tory leader addressed the Commons, stating, "A beacon of freedom has gone. Whatever the disagreement, to me he stood as the embodiment of freedom, not only in his own country, but throughout the world."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990314_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990314-180">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> In the 1964 <a href="/wiki/Great_Canadian_Flag_Debate" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Canadian Flag Debate">Great Canadian Flag Debate</a>, Diefenbaker led the unsuccessful opposition to the <a href="/wiki/Maple_Leaf_flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Maple Leaf flag">Maple Leaf flag</a>, which the Liberals pushed for after the rejection of Pearson's preferred design showing three maple leaves. Diefenbaker preferred the existing <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Red_Ensign" title="Canadian Red Ensign">Canadian Red Ensign</a> or another design showing symbols of the nation's heritage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1977223_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1977223-181">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> He dismissed the adopted design, with a single red maple leaf and two red bars, as "a flag that Peruvians might salute", a reference to <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Peru" title="Flag of Peru">Peru's red-white-red tricolour</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995522–524_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995522–524-182">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> At the request of Quebec Tory <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Balcer" title="Léon Balcer">Léon Balcer</a>, who feared devastating PC losses in the province at the next election, Pearson imposed closure, and the bill passed with the majority singing "<a href="/wiki/O_Canada" title="O Canada">O Canada</a>" as Diefenbaker led the dissenters in "<a href="/wiki/God_Save_the_Queen" class="mw-redirect" title="God Save the Queen">God Save the Queen</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995522–524_182-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995522–524-182">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1966, the Liberals began to make an issue of the <a href="/wiki/Gerda_Munsinger" title="Gerda Munsinger">Munsinger affair</a>—two officials of the Diefenbaker government had slept with a woman suspected of being a Soviet spy. In what Diefenbaker saw as a partisan attack,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1977272_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiefenbaker1977272-183">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Pearson established a one-man <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Commission">Royal Commission</a>, which, according to Diefenbaker biographer Smith, indulged in "three months of reckless political inquisition". By the time the commission issued its report, Diefenbaker and other former ministers had long since withdrawn their counsel from the proceedings. The report faulted Diefenbaker for not dismissing the ministers in question, but found no actual security breach.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995539–541_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995539–541-184">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There were calls for Diefenbaker's retirement, especially from the <a href="/wiki/Bay_Street" title="Bay Street">Bay Street</a> wing of the party as early as 1964. Diefenbaker initially beat back attempts to remove him without trouble.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Dusen196861–62_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Dusen196861–62-185">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> When Pearson called <a href="/wiki/1965_Canadian_federal_election" title="1965 Canadian federal election">an election in 1965</a> in the expectation of receiving a majority, Diefenbaker ran an aggressive campaign. The Liberals fell two seats short of a majority, and the Tories improved their position slightly at the expense of the smaller parties.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995534–536_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995534–536-186">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> After the election, some Tories, led by party president <a href="/wiki/Dalton_Camp" title="Dalton Camp">Dalton Camp</a>, began a quiet campaign to oust Diefenbaker.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the absence of a formal <a href="/wiki/Leadership_review" title="Leadership review">leadership review</a> process, Camp was able to stage a de facto review by running for re-election as party president on the platform of holding a leadership convention within a year. His campaign at the Tories' 1966 convention occurred amidst allegations of vote rigging, violence, and seating arrangements designed to ensure that when Diefenbaker addressed the delegates, television viewers would see unmoved delegates in the first ten rows. Other Camp supporters tried to shout Diefenbaker down. Camp was successful in being re-elected thereby forcing <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1967" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1967">a leadership convention for 1967</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg1976171–176_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg1976171–176-187">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker initially made no announcement as to whether he would stand, but angered by a resolution at the party's policy conference which spoke of <i>"deux nations"</i> or "two founding peoples" (as opposed to Diefenbaker's "One Canada"), decided to seek to retain his leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Although Diefenbaker entered at the last minute to stand as a candidate for the leadership, he finished fifth on each of the first three ballots, and withdrew from the contest, which was won by Nova Scotia Premier <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995558–559_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995558–559-188">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker addressed the delegates before Stanfield spoke:
</p>
<blockquote><p>My course has come to an end. I have fought your battles, and you have given me that loyalty that led us to victory more often than the party has ever had since the days of Sir John A. Macdonald. In my retiring, I have nothing to withdraw in my desire to see Canada, my country and your country, one nation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995559_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995559-189">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_and_death">Final years and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Final years and death"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Diefenbaker was embittered by his loss of the party leadership. Pearson announced his retirement in December 1967, and Diefenbaker forged a wary relationship of mutual respect with Pearson's successor, <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>. Trudeau called <a href="/wiki/1968_Canadian_federal_election" title="1968 Canadian federal election">a general election for June 1968</a>; Stanfield asked Diefenbaker to join him at a rally in Saskatoon, which Diefenbaker refused, although the two appeared at hastily arranged photo opportunities. Trudeau obtained the majority against Stanfield that Pearson had never been able to obtain against Diefenbaker, as the PC party lost 25 seats, 20 of them in the West. The former Prime Minister, though stating, "The Conservative Party has suffered a calamitous disaster" in a <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Canadian Broadcasting Corporation">CBC</a> interview, could not conceal his delight at Stanfield's humiliation, and especially gloated at the defeat of Camp, who made an unsuccessful attempt to enter the Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995559–563_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995559–563-190">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker was easily returned for Prince Albert.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995559–563_190-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995559–563-190">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Stanfield worked to try to unify the party, Diefenbaker and his loyalists proved difficult to reconcile. The division in the party broke out in well-publicised dissensions, as when Diefenbaker called on Progressive Conservative MPs to break with Stanfield's position on <a href="/wiki/Official_Languages_Act_(Canada)" title="Official Languages Act (Canada)">the Official Languages bill</a>, and nearly half the caucus voted against their leader's will or abstained.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995563–564_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995563–564-191">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> In addition to his parliamentary activities, Diefenbaker travelled extensively and began work on his memoirs, which were published in three volumes between 1975 and 1977. Pearson died of cancer in 1972, and Diefenbaker was asked if he had kind words for his old rival. Diefenbaker shook his head and said only, "He shouldn't have won the Nobel Prize."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995565–567_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995565–567-192">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1972, Diefenbaker had grown disillusioned with Trudeau, and campaigned wholeheartedly for the Tories in <a href="/wiki/1972_Canadian_federal_election" title="1972 Canadian federal election">that year's election</a>. Diefenbaker was re-elected comfortably in his home riding, and the Progressive Conservatives came within two seats of matching the Liberal total. Diefenbaker was relieved both that Trudeau had been humbled and that Stanfield had been denied power. Trudeau regained his majority two years later in <a href="/wiki/1974_Canadian_federal_election" title="1974 Canadian federal election">an election</a> that saw Diefenbaker, by then the only living former prime minister, have his personal majority grow to 11,000 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995567–568_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995567–568-193">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1976_New_Year_Honours" title="1976 New Year Honours">1976 New Year Honours</a>, Diefenbaker was created a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of the Companions of Honour">Member of the Order of the Companions of Honour</a>, an honour bestowed as the personal gift of the Sovereign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995568_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995568-194">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> After a long illness, Olive Diefenbaker died on December 22, a loss which plunged Diefenbaker into despair.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995570–571_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995570–571-195">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a> <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1976" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1976">succeeded Stanfield as party leader in 1976</a>, but as Clark had supported the leadership review, Diefenbaker held a grudge against him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995571–574_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995571–574-196">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker had supported <a href="/wiki/Claude_Wagner" title="Claude Wagner">Claude Wagner</a> for leader, but when Clark won, stated that Clark would make "a remarkable leader of this party".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStursberg1976200–201_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStursberg1976200–201-197">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> However, Diefenbaker repeatedly criticized his party leader, to such an extent that Stanfield publicly asked Diefenbaker "to stop sticking a knife into Mr. Clark"—a request Diefenbaker did not agree to.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;The_Canadian_Press&#39;&#39;_1977-09-23_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;The_Canadian_Press&#39;&#39;_1977-09-23-198">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> According to columnist <a href="/wiki/Charles_Lynch_(journalist)" title="Charles Lynch (journalist)">Charles Lynch</a>, Diefenbaker regarded Clark as an upstart and a pipsqueak.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELynch1977_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELynch1977-199">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Diefengrave.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A grave marked by an angled slab of marble engraved with the names of Diefenbaker and his wife and surrounded by plantings of small marigolds, and an additional plaque in bronze. Beyond it a lawn stretches away." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Diefengrave.jpg/220px-Diefengrave.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Diefengrave.jpg/330px-Diefengrave.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Diefengrave.jpg/440px-Diefengrave.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption>The Diefenbaker grave, close by the <a href="/wiki/Diefenbaker_Centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Diefenbaker Centre">Diefenbaker Centre</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1978, Diefenbaker announced that he would stand in one more election, and under the slogan "Diefenbaker—Now More Than Ever", weathered a campaign the following year during which he apparently suffered a mild stroke, although the media were told he was bedridden with influenza. In <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_election" title="1979 Canadian federal election">the May election</a> Diefenbaker defeated NDP candidate <a href="/wiki/Stan_Hovdebo" title="Stan Hovdebo">Stan Hovdebo</a> (who, after Diefenbaker's death, would win the seat in a by-election) by 4,000 votes. Clark had defeated Trudeau, though only gaining a minority government, and Diefenbaker returned to Ottawa to witness the swearing-in, still unreconciled to his old opponents among Clark's ministers. Two months later, Diefenbaker died of a heart attack in his study at age 83.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995571–574_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995571–574-196">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker had extensively planned his funeral in consultation with government officials. He <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state" title="Lying in state">lay in state</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Hall_of_Honour" class="mw-redirect" title="Hall of Honour">Hall of Honour</a> in Parliament for two and a half days; 10,000 Canadians passed by his casket. The Maple Leaf Flag on the casket was partially obscured by the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Red_Ensign" title="Canadian Red Ensign">Red Ensign</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995575–577_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995575–577-200">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEArchbold2002147_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArchbold2002147-201">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> After the service, his body was taken by train on a slow journey to its final destination, Saskatoon; along the route, many Canadians lined the tracks to watch the funeral train pass. In Winnipeg, an estimated 10,000 people waited at midnight in a one-kilometre line to file past the casket which made the trip draped in a Canadian flag and Diefenbaker's beloved Red Ensign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;CBC_Digital_Archives&#39;&#39;,_Farewell_Dief_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;CBC_Digital_Archives&#39;&#39;,_Farewell_Dief-202">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> In Prince Albert, thousands of those he had represented filled the square in front of the railroad station to salute the only man from Saskatchewan ever to become Prime Minister. His coffin was accompanied by that of his wife Olive, disinterred from temporary burial in Ottawa. Prime Minister Clark delivered the eulogy, paying tribute to "an indomitable man, born to a minority group, raised in a minority region, leader of a minority party, who went on to change the very nature of his country, and change it forever".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995575–577_200-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995575–577-200">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> John and Olive Diefenbaker rest outside the <a href="/wiki/Diefenbaker_Centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Diefenbaker Centre">Diefenbaker Centre</a>, built to house his papers, on the campus of the University of Saskatchewan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995575–577_200-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995575–577-200">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Parks_Canada&#39;&#39;,_Historic_Sites_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE&#39;&#39;Parks_Canada&#39;&#39;,_Historic_Sites-203">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Legacy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Bronze statue of Diefenbaker, taken from one side. He is depicted wearing an overcoat over a suit. He carries the Bill of Rights under his arm." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg/170px-John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="254" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg/255px-John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg/340px-John_Diefenbaker_Statue.jpg 2x" data-file-width="648" data-file-height="968" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Leo_Mol" title="Leo Mol">Leo Mol</a>'s statue of John Diefenbaker on Parliament Hill, Ottawa</figcaption></figure> 
<p>Patrick Kyba and Wendy Green-Finlay report:
</p>
<blockquote><p> Most of those who have written about John Diefenbaker have concluded that he was a failure as a leader, or that, while he may have had some successes, he never fully lived up to his promise and, therefore, must be considered a failure.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Historian <a href="/wiki/Conrad_Black" title="Conrad Black">Conrad Black</a> says that Diefenbaker:
</p>
<blockquote><p> was not a successful prime minister; he was a jumble of attitudes but had little in the way of policy, was a disorganized administrator, and was inconsistent, indecisive, and not infrequently irrational. But he was very formidable; a deadly campaigner, an idiosyncratic but often galvanizing public speaker, a brilliant parliamentarian, and a man of many fine qualities. He was absolutely honest financially, a passionate supporter of the average and the underprivileged and disadvantaged person, a fierce opponent of any racial or religious or socioeconomic discrimination....<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> </p></blockquote>
<p>According to <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bothwell" title="Robert Bothwell">Robert Bothwell</a>:
</p>
<blockquote><p>By the time Diefenbaker left office, his conduct of foreign policy was reviled by an important and growing number of Canadians, while his relations with both the Americans and the British were disastrous. Canada's influence in the world was declining....Overseas, at Canada's NATO garrisons, the prime minister's portrait was used as a dartboard in military messes.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Some of Diefenbaker's policies did not survive the 16 years of Liberal government that followed his fall. By the end of 1963, the first of the Bomarc warheads entered Canada, where they remained until the last were finally phased out during <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>'s brief government in 1984.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990310_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990310-207">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker's decision to have Canada remain outside the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">OAS</a> was not reversed by Pearson, and it was not until 1989, under the Tory government of <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a>, that Canada joined.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENash1990114_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1990114-208">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>But several defining features of modern Canada can be traced back to Diefenbaker. Diefenbaker's Bill of Rights remains in effect, and signalled the change in Canadian political culture that would eventually bring about the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms" title="Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms">Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms</a>, which came into force after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker has had several locations named in his honour, some before his 1979 death (particularly in his home province of Saskatchewan, including <a href="/wiki/Lake_Diefenbaker" title="Lake Diefenbaker">Lake Diefenbaker</a>, the largest lake in Southern Saskatchewan, and the <a href="/wiki/Diefenbaker_Bridge" title="Diefenbaker Bridge">Diefenbaker Bridge</a> in Prince Albert), others after (in 1993, Saskatoon renamed its airport the <a href="/wiki/Saskatoon_John_G._Diefenbaker_International_Airport" title="Saskatoon John G. Diefenbaker International Airport">Saskatoon John G. Diefenbaker International Airport</a>). The city of Prince Albert continues to maintain the house he resided in from 1947 to 1975 as a public museum known as <a href="/wiki/Diefenbaker_House" title="Diefenbaker House">Diefenbaker House</a>; it was designated a <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Sites_of_Canada" title="National Historic Sites of Canada">National Historic Site</a> in 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Diefenbaker reinvigorated a moribund party system in Canada. Clark and Mulroney, two men who, as students, worked on and were inspired by his 1957 triumph, became the only other Progressive Conservatives to lead the party to election triumphs.<sup id="cite_ref-Kim_Campbell_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kim_Campbell-210">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1995568_194-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1995568-194">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker's biographer, Denis Smith, wrote of him, "In politics he had little more than two years of success in the midst of failure and frustration, but he retained a core of deeply committed loyalists to the end of his life and beyond. The federal Conservative Party that he had revived remained dominant in the prairie provinces for 25 years after he left the leadership."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith2016_17-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2016-17">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Prime_Ministership_of_Stephen_Harper" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Ministership of Stephen Harper">Harper government</a>, believing that Tory prime ministers have been given short shrift in the naming of Canadian places and institutions, named the former Ottawa City Hall, now a federal office building, the <a href="/wiki/John_G._Diefenbaker_Building" title="John G. Diefenbaker Building">John G. Diefenbaker Building</a>. It also gave Diefenbaker's name to a human rights award and <a href="/wiki/CCGS_John_G._Diefenbaker" title="CCGS John G. Diefenbaker">an icebreaking vessel</a>. <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Harper</a> often invoked Diefenbaker's northern vision in his speeches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECheadle2011_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheadle2011-211">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Conservative Senator <a href="/wiki/Marjory_LeBreton" title="Marjory LeBreton">Marjory LeBreton</a> worked in Diefenbaker's office during his second time as Opposition Leader, and has said of him, "He brought a lot of firsts to Canada, but a lot of it has been air-brushed from history by those who followed."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECobb2011_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECobb2011-212">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/wiki/Michael_Bliss" title="Michael Bliss">Michael Bliss</a>, who published a survey of the Canadian Prime Ministers, wrote of Diefenbaker:
</p>
<blockquote><p>From the distance of our times, Diefenbaker's role as a prairie populist who tried to revolutionize the Conservative Party begins to loom larger than his personal idiosyncrasies. The difficulties he faced in the form of significant historical dilemmas seem less easy to resolve than Liberals and hostile journalists opined at the time. If Diefenbaker defies rehabilitation, he can at least be appreciated. He stood for a fascinating and still relevant combination of individual and egalitarian values&#160;... But his contemporaries were also right in seeing some kind of disorder near the centre of his personality and his prime-ministership. The problems of leadership, authority, power, ego, and a mad time in history overwhelmed the prairie politician with the odd name.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBliss2004186_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBliss2004186-213">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honorary_degrees">Honorary degrees</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Diefenbaker&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Honorary degrees"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
